Yeah, uh… I guess the main thing is people keep telling me I’m not listening. Like, not just once in a while—my supervisor at work will be standing right there talking straight to me, explaining something, and I’m looking at him like I’m hearing him, but then a minute later I realize I only caught pieces of it. Then I’m asking him to repeat himself, and it looks bad, like I don’t care or I’m ignoring him.

And it’s the same at home with my wife. She’ll tell me something directly, and I honestly think I’m paying attention, but then afterward I realize I missed half of what she said. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be rude. It’s like the words are coming in, but they don’t all stick, if that makes sense. That’s really what pushed me to come in.

Yeah, definitely. That’s actually one of the things people notice about me all the time. If I’m sitting in one of those safety huddles at work, you know, where everyone’s supposed to just stand or sit there and listen, my leg is going the whole time. Like bouncing nonstop. And if I’m sitting down, I’ll be messing with a pen, clicking it, turning it around in my fingers, or I’ve got my keys in my hand and I don’t even realize I’m jingling them.

Same thing at dinner, honestly. I’ll be at the table with my wife and my leg’s bouncing under the table, and she’ll be like, “Can you stop? You’re shaking everything.” Or if we’re sitting on a bench somewhere, people tell me I’m moving it because my foot keeps going. I don’t always notice I’m doing it until somebody points it out, but once they do, I realize I’ve been moving the whole time. It’s like sitting still takes effort, if that makes sense.

Then I’m asking him to repeat himself, and it looks bad, like I don’t care or I’m ignoring him.\n\nAnd it’s the same at home with my wife. She’ll tell me something directly, and I honestly think I’m paying attention, but then afterward I realize I missed half of what she said. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be rude. That’s really what pushed me to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting, restless, or having trouble sitting still, especially in situations where you're supposed to be quiet or focused?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. That’s actually one of the things people notice about me all the time. If I’m sitting in one of those safety huddles at work, you know, where everyone’s supposed to just stand or sit there and listen, my leg is going the whole time. Like bouncing nonstop. And if I’m sitting down, I’ll be messing with a pen, clicking it, turning it around in my fingers, or I’ve got my keys in my hand and I don’t even realize I’m jingling them.\n\nSame thing at dinner, honestly. I’ll be at the table with my wife and my leg’s bouncing under the table, and she’ll be like, “Can you stop? You’re shaking everything.” Or if we’re sitting on a bench somewhere, people tell me I’m moving it because my foot keeps going. Then I’m asking him to repeat himself, and it looks bad, like I don’t care or I’m ignoring him.\n\nAnd it’s the same at home with my wife. She’ll tell me something directly, and I honestly think I’m paying attention, but then afterward I realize I missed half of what she said. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be rude. It’s like the words are coming in, but they don’t all stick, if that makes sense. That’s really what pushed me to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting, restless, or having trouble sitting still, especially in situations where you're supposed to be quiet or focused?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. That’s actually one of the things people notice about me all the time. If I’m sitting in one of those safety huddles at work, you know, where everyone’s supposed to just stand or sit there and listen, my leg is going the whole time. Like bouncing nonstop. And if I’m sitting down, I’ll be messing with a pen, clicking it, turning it around in my fingers, or I’ve got my keys in my hand and I don’t even realize I’m jingling them.\n\nSame thing at dinner, honestly. I’ll be at the table with my wife and my leg’s bouncing under the table, and she’ll be like, “Can you stop? You’re shaking everything.” Or if we’re sitting on a bench somewhere, people tell me I’m moving it because my foot keeps going. I don’t always notice I’m doing it until somebody points it out, but once they do, I realize I’ve been moving the whole time. It’s like sitting still takes effort, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble completing tasks or projects?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that one. I mean, everybody has stuff they don’t finish sometimes, right? If you mean like big projects where I just abandon them halfway through, I don’t know that I’ve really noticed a clear pattern like that.\n\nAt work, most things kind of have to get done because the line is moving or there’s a schedule, so it’s not like I can just walk away from it. And at home… I mean, I guess there are normal things that sit around sometimes, but I can’t say for sure it’s a major thing I’ve paid attention to. I’d have to think about what you mean exactly by “tasks or projects.”\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you have trouble organizing your time or activities?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I’m not sure, honestly. Like, I don’t know if I’d call it “organizing my time” exactly. My days are pretty set because of work, so I kind of just go by the schedule that’s already there. It’s like sitting still takes effort, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble completing tasks or projects?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that one. I mean, everybody has stuff they don’t finish sometimes, right? If you mean like big projects where I just abandon them halfway through, I don’t know that I’ve really noticed a clear pattern like that.\n\nAt work, most things kind of have to get done because the line is moving or there’s a schedule, so it’s not like I can just walk away from it. And at home… I mean, I guess there are normal things that sit around sometimes, but I can’t say for sure it’s a major thing I’ve paid attention to. I’d have to think about what you mean exactly by “tasks or projects.”\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your time or activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Like, I don’t know if I’d call it “organizing my time” exactly. My days are pretty set because of work, so I kind of just go by the schedule that’s already there. If I’m supposed to be on the line or at a certain station, that sort of decides what I’m doing, so I’m not really sitting down and planning things out.\n\nAt home, I mean… I guess we have normal stuff, errands and chores and things like that, but I haven’t really thought of it as a big issue. My wife probably has a better idea of that than I do, because she pays more attention to the calendar and that kind of thing. I don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure. I guess I’d need you to explain what you mean—like being late, or forgetting plans, or not knowing what to do first? Because I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear pattern with that.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they've finished speaking?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 42 / production_worker / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Blurting_Answers\nmanifestation: \"In meetings I answer before the supervisor finishes the question, and sometimes I’m already talking before I realize I didn’t let him get to the important part.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yes, I do that. Probably more than I’d like to admit. In meetings at work especially, like when the supervisor is asking something, I’ll jump in before he’s even finished the whole question. I’ll think I know where he’s going with it, and my mouth just kind of starts before I’ve really waited.

And then sometimes I realize halfway through that he hadn’t even gotten to the important part yet. Like I’m already answering the first half, and then he adds something that changes what he was asking, and I feel kind of dumb because I basically cut him off for no reason. It’s not usually me trying to be rude or take over, it’s more like the answer is sitting right there and I feel this urge to get it out before I lose it or before somebody else says something. But yeah, I do interrupt that way.

You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I do. Not just in conversations, but at work especially, with activities or jobs on the line. I have a hard time waiting until it’s actually my turn to step in.

Like if I see a coworker setting something up and I think they’re doing it too slow or not the way I would do it, I’ll just kind of reach in and start adjusting it, or I’ll tell them, “No, no, do it this way,” before they’ve asked me for help. And then it turns into a thing because, honestly, they’re right to get annoyed. It’s their station, their job at that moment, and I’m cutting into it like I’m in charge or like I can’t just wait and let them finish.

It’s not that I’m trying to start arguments. In my head it feels like, “I see the problem, I can fix it quick,” and then I’m already doing it before I’ve really thought about whether I should. But yeah, it causes problems. People snap at me, or I snap back, and then it becomes an argument because I didn’t wait my turn or stay out of it.

It’s like the words are coming in, but they don’t all stick, if that makes sense. That’s actually one of the things people notice about me all the time. And if I’m sitting down, I’ll be messing with a pen, clicking it, turning it around in my fingers, or I’ve got my keys in my hand and I don’t even realize I’m jingling them.\n\nSame thing at dinner, honestly. I’ll be at the table with my wife and my leg’s bouncing under the table, and she’ll be like, “Can you stop?   {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’d say it’s more the impulse side, if I’m being honest. I mean, I can have trouble with details here and there like anybody, but the thing that gets me in trouble the most is not stopping myself before I jump in.

Like at work, if I see somebody doing a setup and I think, “That’s not right,” or “I can do that faster,” I don’t always wait for them to ask or finish what they’re doing. I’ll just reach in and start fixing something, or I’ll tell them how to do it while they’re still in the middle of doing their own job. And I know how that sounds — it sounds bossy, and it probably feels disrespectful to them — but in the moment it doesn’t feel like I’m trying to take over. It feels more like my body is already moving before I’ve had time to think, “Hold on, this isn’t your station, let them handle it.”

That’s where arguments start. They’ll say, “Back off, I’ve got it,” or get mad because I’m in their space, and then I get defensive because I feel like I was just trying to help or fix it quickly. But really, the issue is I didn’t wait my turn. I cut into what they were doing without being asked. So yeah, for me it’s more controlling that urge to jump in and act than it is following instructions.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, uh… I guess the main thing is people keep telling me I’m not listening. Like, not just once in a while—my supervisor at work will be standing right there talking straight to me, explaining something, and I’m looking at him like I’m hearing him, but then a minute later I realize I only caught pieces of it. Then I’m asking him to repeat himself, and it looks bad, like I don’t care or I’m ignoring him.\n\nAnd it’s the same at home with my wife. She’ll tell me something directly, and I honestly think I’m paying attention, but then afterward I realize I missed half of what she said. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be rude. It’s like the words are coming in, but they don’t all stick, if that makes sense. That’s really what pushed me to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting, restless, or having trouble sitting still, especially in situations where you're supposed to be quiet or focused?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. That’s actually one of the things people notice about me all the time. If I’m sitting in one of those safety huddles at work, you know, where everyone’s supposed to just stand or sit there and listen, my leg is going the whole time. Like bouncing nonstop. And if I’m sitting down, I’ll be messing with a pen, clicking it, turning it around in my fingers, or I’ve got my keys in my hand and I don’t even realize I’m jingling them.\n\nSame thing at dinner, honestly. I’ll be at the table with my wife and my leg’s bouncing under the table, and she’ll be like, “Can you stop? You’re shaking everything.” Or if we’re sitting on a bench somewhere, people tell me I’m moving it because my foot keeps going. I don’t always notice I’m doing it until somebody points it out, but once they do, I realize I’ve been moving the whole time. It’s like sitting still takes effort, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble completing tasks or projects?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that one. I mean, everybody has stuff they don’t finish sometimes, right? If you mean like big projects where I just abandon them halfway through, I don’t know that I’ve really noticed a clear pattern like that.\n\nAt work, most things kind of have to get done because the line is moving or there’s a schedule, so it’s not like I can just walk away from it. And at home… I mean, I guess there are normal things that sit around sometimes, but I can’t say for sure it’s a major thing I’ve paid attention to. I’d have to think about what you mean exactly by “tasks or projects.”\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your time or activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Like, I don’t know if I’d call it “organizing my time” exactly. My days are pretty set because of work, so I kind of just go by the schedule that’s already there. If I’m supposed to be on the line or at a certain station, that sort of decides what I’m doing, so I’m not really sitting down and planning things out.\n\nAt home, I mean… I guess we have normal stuff, errands and chores and things like that, but I haven’t really thought of it as a big issue. My wife probably has a better idea of that than I do, because she pays more attention to the calendar and that kind of thing. I don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure. I guess I’d need you to explain what you mean—like being late, or forgetting plans, or not knowing what to do first? Because I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear pattern with that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they've finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I do that. Probably more than I’d like to admit. In meetings at work especially, like when the supervisor is asking something, I’ll jump in before he’s even finished the whole question. I’ll think I know where he’s going with it, and my mouth just kind of starts before I’ve really waited.\n\nAnd then sometimes I realize halfway through that he hadn’t even gotten to the important part yet. Like I’m already answering the first half, and then he adds something that changes what he was asking, and I feel kind of dumb because I basically cut him off for no reason. It’s not usually me trying to be rude or take over, it’s more like the answer is sitting right there and I feel this urge to get it out before I lose it or before somebody else says something. But yeah, I do interrupt that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting for your turn in conversations or activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not just in conversations, but at work especially, with activities or jobs on the line. I have a hard time waiting until it’s actually my turn to step in.\n\nLike if I see a coworker setting something up and I think they’re doing it too slow or not the way I would do it, I’ll just kind of reach in and start adjusting it, or I’ll tell them, “No, no, do it this way,” before they’ve asked me for help. And then it turns into a thing because, honestly, they’re right to get annoyed. It’s their station, their job at that moment, and I’m cutting into it like I’m in charge or like I can’t just wait and let them finish.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to start arguments. In my head it feels like, “I see the problem, I can fix it quick,” and then I’m already doing it before I’ve really thought about whether I should. But yeah, it causes problems. People snap at me, or I snap back, and then it becomes an argument because I didn’t wait my turn or stay out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is more with paying attention to details and following through on instructions, or with controlling your impulses and activities?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more the impulse side, if I’m being honest. I mean, I can have trouble with details here and there like anybody, but the thing that gets me in trouble the most is not stopping myself before I jump in.\n\nLike at work, if I see somebody doing a setup and I think, “That’s not right,” or “I can do that faster,” I don’t always wait for them to ask or finish what they’re doing. I’ll just reach in and start fixing something, or I’ll tell them how to do it while they’re still in the middle of doing their own job. And I know how that sounds — it sounds bossy, and it probably feels disrespectful to them — but in the moment it doesn’t feel like I’m trying to take over. It feels more like my body is already moving before I’ve had time to think, “Hold on, this isn’t your station, let them handle it.”\n\nThat’s where arguments start. They’ll say, “Back off, I’ve got it,” or get mad because I’m in their space, and then I get defensive because I feel like I was just trying to help or fix it quickly. People snap at me, or I snap back, and then it becomes an argument because I didn’t wait my turn or stay out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is more with paying attention to details and following through on instructions, or with controlling your impulses and activities?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more the impulse side, if I’m being honest. I’ll just reach in and start fixing something, or I’ll tell them how to do it while they’re still in the middle of doing their own job. And I know how that sounds — it sounds bossy, and it probably feels disrespectful to them — but in the moment it doesn’t feel like I’m trying to take over. It feels more like my body is already moving before I’ve had time to think, “Hold on, this isn’t your station, let them handle it.”\n\nThat’s where arguments start. They’ll say, “Back off, I’ve got it,” or get mad because I’m in their space, and then I get defensive because I feel like I was just trying to help or fix it quickly. But really, the issue is I didn’t wait my turn. I cut into what they were doing without being asked. So yeah, for me it’s more controlling that urge to jump in and act than it is following instructions.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you have more trouble sitting still and being patient, or do you have more trouble with forgetting things or making careless mistakes?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 42 / production_worker / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Feeling_Restless\nmanifestation: \"When I have to sit still, it feels like pressure building up inside my chest and legs, like I’ve got to move or do something right now or I’ll crawl out of my skin.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Definitely more trouble with sitting still and being patient. I mean, I do make mistakes sometimes, sure, but the thing I really feel in my body is when I’m supposed to just sit there and wait.

It’s like this pressure starts building up inside me, especially in my chest and my legs. My legs feel like they need to move, and my chest gets this tight, restless feeling, like there’s energy trapped in there. And it’s not just “I’m a little bored.” It’s more like, if I don’t move or do something right now, I’m going to crawl out of my skin.

So if I’m sitting somewhere and I’m supposed to be calm, I’m usually fighting that feeling the whole time. I’m thinking about how much longer I have to stay there, or when I can get up, or what I can do with my hands or my feet. Being patient feels like holding something back physically, not just mentally.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, uh… I guess the main thing is people keep telling me I’m not listening. Like, not just once in a while—my supervisor at work will be standing right there talking straight to me, explaining something, and I’m looking at him like I’m hearing him, but then a minute later I realize I only caught pieces of it. Then I’m asking him to repeat himself, and it looks bad, like I don’t care or I’m ignoring him.\n\nAnd it’s the same at home with my wife. She’ll tell me something directly, and I honestly think I’m paying attention, but then afterward I realize I missed half of what she said. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be rude. It’s like the words are coming in, but they don’t all stick, if that makes sense. That’s really what pushed me to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting, restless, or having trouble sitting still, especially in situations where you're supposed to be quiet or focused?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. That’s actually one of the things people notice about me all the time. If I’m sitting in one of those safety huddles at work, you know, where everyone’s supposed to just stand or sit there and listen, my leg is going the whole time. Like bouncing nonstop. And if I’m sitting down, I’ll be messing with a pen, clicking it, turning it around in my fingers, or I’ve got my keys in my hand and I don’t even realize I’m jingling them.\n\nSame thing at dinner, honestly. I’ll be at the table with my wife and my leg’s bouncing under the table, and she’ll be like, “Can you stop? You’re shaking everything.” Or if we’re sitting on a bench somewhere, people tell me I’m moving it because my foot keeps going. I don’t always notice I’m doing it until somebody points it out, but once they do, I realize I’ve been moving the whole time. It’s like sitting still takes effort, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble completing tasks or projects?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that one. I mean, everybody has stuff they don’t finish sometimes, right? If you mean like big projects where I just abandon them halfway through, I don’t know that I’ve really noticed a clear pattern like that.\n\nAt work, most things kind of have to get done because the line is moving or there’s a schedule, so it’s not like I can just walk away from it. And at home… I mean, I guess there are normal things that sit around sometimes, but I can’t say for sure it’s a major thing I’ve paid attention to. I’d have to think about what you mean exactly by “tasks or projects.”\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your time or activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Like, I don’t know if I’d call it “organizing my time” exactly. My days are pretty set because of work, so I kind of just go by the schedule that’s already there. If I’m supposed to be on the line or at a certain station, that sort of decides what I’m doing, so I’m not really sitting down and planning things out.\n\nAt home, I mean… I guess we have normal stuff, errands and chores and things like that, but I haven’t really thought of it as a big issue. My wife probably has a better idea of that than I do, because she pays more attention to the calendar and that kind of thing. I don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure. I guess I’d need you to explain what you mean—like being late, or forgetting plans, or not knowing what to do first? Because I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear pattern with that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they've finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I do that. Probably more than I’d like to admit. In meetings at work especially, like when the supervisor is asking something, I’ll jump in before he’s even finished the whole question. I’ll think I know where he’s going with it, and my mouth just kind of starts before I’ve really waited.\n\nAnd then sometimes I realize halfway through that he hadn’t even gotten to the important part yet. Like I’m already answering the first half, and then he adds something that changes what he was asking, and I feel kind of dumb because I basically cut him off for no reason. It’s not usually me trying to be rude or take over, it’s more like the answer is sitting right there and I feel this urge to get it out before I lose it or before somebody else says something. But yeah, I do interrupt that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting for your turn in conversations or activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not just in conversations, but at work especially, with activities or jobs on the line. I have a hard time waiting until it’s actually my turn to step in.\n\nLike if I see a coworker setting something up and I think they’re doing it too slow or not the way I would do it, I’ll just kind of reach in and start adjusting it, or I’ll tell them, “No, no, do it this way,” before they’ve asked me for help. And then it turns into a thing because, honestly, they’re right to get annoyed. It’s their station, their job at that moment, and I’m cutting into it like I’m in charge or like I can’t just wait and let them finish.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to start arguments. In my head it feels like, “I see the problem, I can fix it quick,” and then I’m already doing it before I’ve really thought about whether I should. But yeah, it causes problems. People snap at me, or I snap back, and then it becomes an argument because I didn’t wait my turn or stay out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is more with paying attention to details and following through on instructions, or with controlling your impulses and activities?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more the impulse side, if I’m being honest. I mean, I can have trouble with details here and there like anybody, but the thing that gets me in trouble the most is not stopping myself before I jump in.\n\nLike at work, if I see somebody doing a setup and I think, “That’s not right,” or “I can do that faster,” I don’t always wait for them to ask or finish what they’re doing. I’ll just reach in and start fixing something, or I’ll tell them how to do it while they’re still in the middle of doing their own job. And I know how that sounds — it sounds bossy, and it probably feels disrespectful to them — but in the moment it doesn’t feel like I’m trying to take over. It feels more like my body is already moving before I’ve had time to think, “Hold on, this isn’t your station, let them handle it.”\n\nThat’s where arguments start. They’ll say, “Back off, I’ve got it,” or get mad because I’m in their space, and then I get defensive because I feel like I was just trying to help or fix it quickly. But really, the issue is I didn’t wait my turn. I cut into what they were doing without being asked. I mean, I do make mistakes sometimes, sure, but the thing I really feel in my body is when I’m supposed to just sit there and wait.\n\nIt’s like this pressure starts building up inside me, especially in my chest and my legs. My legs feel like they need to move, and my chest gets this tight, restless feeling, like there’s energy trapped in there. And it’s not just “I’m a little bored.” It’s more like, if I don’t move or do something right now, I’m going to crawl out of my skin.\n\nSo if I’m sitting somewhere and I’m supposed to be calm, I’m usually fighting that feeling the whole time. I’m thinking about how much longer I have to stay there, or when I can get up, or what I can do with my hands or my feet. Being patient feels like holding something back physically, not just mentally.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often find yourself getting easily frustrated or angry in situations?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (Predominantly Hyperactive/Impulsive Presentation), in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting for your turn in conversations or activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not just in conversations, but at work especially, with activities or jobs on the line. I have a hard time waiting until it’s actually my turn to step in.\n\nLike if I see a coworker setting something up and I think they’re doing it too slow or not the way I would do it, I’ll just kind of reach in and start adjusting it, or I’ll tell them, “No, no, do it this way,” before they’ve asked me for help. And then it turns into a thing because, honestly, they’re right to get annoyed. It’s their station, their job at that moment, and I’m cutting into it like I’m in charge or like I can’t just wait and let them finish.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to start arguments. In my head it feels like, “I see the problem, I can fix it quick,” and then I’m already doing it before I’ve really thought about whether I should. But yeah, it causes problems. People snap at me, or I snap back, and then it becomes an argument because I didn’t wait my turn or stay out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is more with paying attention to details and following through on instructions, or with controlling your impulses and activities?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more the impulse side, if I’m being honest. I mean, I can have trouble with details here and there like anybody, but the thing that gets me in trouble the most is not stopping myself before I jump in.\n\nLike at work, if I see somebody doing a setup and I think, “That’s not right,” or “I can do that faster,” I don’t always wait for them to ask or finish what they’re doing. I’ll just reach in and start fixing something, or I’ll tell them how to do it while they’re still in the middle of doing their own job. And I know how that sounds — it sounds bossy, and it probably feels disrespectful to them — but in the moment it doesn’t feel like I’m trying to take over. It feels more like my body is already moving before I’ve had time to think, “Hold on, this isn’t your station, let them handle it.”\n\nThat’s where arguments start. They’ll say, “Back off, I’ve got it,” or get mad because I’m in their space, and then I get defensive because I feel like I was just trying to help or fix it quickly. But really, the issue is I didn’t wait my turn. I cut into what they were doing without being asked. So yeah, for me it’s more controlling that urge to jump in and act than it is following instructions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have more trouble sitting still and being patient, or do you have more trouble with forgetting things or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: Definitely more trouble with sitting still and being patient. I mean, I do make mistakes sometimes, sure, but the thing I really feel in my body is when I’m supposed to just sit there and wait.\n\nIt’s like this pressure starts building up inside me, especially in my chest and my legs. My legs feel like they need to move, and my chest gets this tight, restless feeling, like there’s energy trapped in there. And it’s not just “I’m a little bored.” It’s more like, if I don’t move or do something right now, I’m going to crawl out of my skin.\n\nSo if I’m sitting somewhere and I’m supposed to be calm, I’m usually fighting that feeling the whole time. I’m thinking about how much longer I have to stay there, or when I can get up, or what I can do with my hands or my feet. Being patient feels like holding something back physically, not just mentally.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often find yourself getting easily frustrated or angry in situations?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 42 / production_worker / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure how to answer that one. I mean, I do get annoyed like anyone does, especially if something feels like it’s dragging on or not going the way I think it should, but I don’t know if I’d say I’m angry all the time or anything like that.

When you say “easily frustrated,” I guess I’d need to think about what you mean exactly. Like, frustrated in the moment, yeah, that can happen. But I don’t know if I’ve really paid attention to it as its own thing, separate from just being impatient or wanting something to move along. Sometimes it’s hard for me to tell if I’m actually mad, or if I’m just wound up and reacting too fast.

So… I guess I’d say maybe, but I’m not sure. I haven’t really tracked it that way.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, uh… I guess the main thing is people keep telling me I’m not listening. Like, not just once in a while—my supervisor at work will be standing right there talking straight to me, explaining something, and I’m looking at him like I’m hearing him, but then a minute later I realize I only caught pieces of it. Then I’m asking him to repeat himself, and it looks bad, like I don’t care or I’m ignoring him.\n\nAnd it’s the same at home with my wife. She’ll tell me something directly, and I honestly think I’m paying attention, but then afterward I realize I missed half of what she said. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be rude. It’s like the words are coming in, but they don’t all stick, if that makes sense. That’s really what pushed me to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting, restless, or having trouble sitting still, especially in situations where you're supposed to be quiet or focused?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. That’s actually one of the things people notice about me all the time. If I’m sitting in one of those safety huddles at work, you know, where everyone’s supposed to just stand or sit there and listen, my leg is going the whole time. Like bouncing nonstop. And if I’m sitting down, I’ll be messing with a pen, clicking it, turning it around in my fingers, or I’ve got my keys in my hand and I don’t even realize I’m jingling them.\n\nSame thing at dinner, honestly. I’ll be at the table with my wife and my leg’s bouncing under the table, and she’ll be like, “Can you stop? You’re shaking everything.” Or if we’re sitting on a bench somewhere, people tell me I’m moving it because my foot keeps going. I don’t always notice I’m doing it until somebody points it out, but once they do, I realize I’ve been moving the whole time. It’s like sitting still takes effort, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble completing tasks or projects?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that one. I mean, everybody has stuff they don’t finish sometimes, right? If you mean like big projects where I just abandon them halfway through, I don’t know that I’ve really noticed a clear pattern like that.\n\nAt work, most things kind of have to get done because the line is moving or there’s a schedule, so it’s not like I can just walk away from it. And at home… I mean, I guess there are normal things that sit around sometimes, but I can’t say for sure it’s a major thing I’ve paid attention to. I’d have to think about what you mean exactly by “tasks or projects.”\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your time or activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Like, I don’t know if I’d call it “organizing my time” exactly. My days are pretty set because of work, so I kind of just go by the schedule that’s already there. If I’m supposed to be on the line or at a certain station, that sort of decides what I’m doing, so I’m not really sitting down and planning things out.\n\nAt home, I mean… I guess we have normal stuff, errands and chores and things like that, but I haven’t really thought of it as a big issue. My wife probably has a better idea of that than I do, because she pays more attention to the calendar and that kind of thing. I don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure. I guess I’d need you to explain what you mean—like being late, or forgetting plans, or not knowing what to do first? Because I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear pattern with that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they've finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I do that. Probably more than I’d like to admit. In meetings at work especially, like when the supervisor is asking something, I’ll jump in before he’s even finished the whole question. I’ll think I know where he’s going with it, and my mouth just kind of starts before I’ve really waited.\n\nAnd then sometimes I realize halfway through that he hadn’t even gotten to the important part yet. Like I’m already answering the first half, and then he adds something that changes what he was asking, and I feel kind of dumb because I basically cut him off for no reason. It’s not usually me trying to be rude or take over, it’s more like the answer is sitting right there and I feel this urge to get it out before I lose it or before somebody else says something. But yeah, I do interrupt that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting for your turn in conversations or activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not just in conversations, but at work especially, with activities or jobs on the line. I have a hard time waiting until it’s actually my turn to step in.\n\nLike if I see a coworker setting something up and I think they’re doing it too slow or not the way I would do it, I’ll just kind of reach in and start adjusting it, or I’ll tell them, “No, no, do it this way,” before they’ve asked me for help. And then it turns into a thing because, honestly, they’re right to get annoyed. It’s their station, their job at that moment, and I’m cutting into it like I’m in charge or like I can’t just wait and let them finish.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to start arguments. In my head it feels like, “I see the problem, I can fix it quick,” and then I’m already doing it before I’ve really thought about whether I should. But yeah, it causes problems. People snap at me, or I snap back, and then it becomes an argument because I didn’t wait my turn or stay out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is more with paying attention to details and following through on instructions, or with controlling your impulses and activities?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more the impulse side, if I’m being honest. I mean, I can have trouble with details here and there like anybody, but the thing that gets me in trouble the most is not stopping myself before I jump in.\n\nLike at work, if I see somebody doing a setup and I think, “That’s not right,” or “I can do that faster,” I don’t always wait for them to ask or finish what they’re doing. I’ll just reach in and start fixing something, or I’ll tell them how to do it while they’re still in the middle of doing their own job. (Note: In adolescents or adults, may be limited to feeling restless.)\n  manifestation: When I have to sit still, it feels like pressure building up inside my chest and legs, like I’ve got to move or do something right now or I’ll crawl out of my skin.\n- key: S014\n  name: Constant_Activity\n  description: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n  manifestation: From the time I clock in until I get home, I’m always jumping to the next thing—work orders, pickup, dinner, laundry—and even when there’s a quiet minute I can’t just sit down and rest.\n- key: S016\n  name: Blurting_Answers\n  description: Often blurts out an answer before a question has been completed.\n  manifestation: In meetings I answer before the supervisor finishes the question, and sometimes I’m already talking before I realize I didn’t let him get to the important part.\n- key: S018\n  name: Interrupting_Intruding\n  description: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n  manifestation: I cut into coworkers’ jobs without being asked, like reaching in to fix their setup or telling them how to do it, and it causes arguments because I don’t wait my turn.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: I was like this even as a kid—teachers were always moving my seat because I couldn’t stay put, called out answers, and got into trouble for butting into other kids’ work before middle school.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: At work it has gotten serious enough that I’ve had formal warnings, and I worry I could lose my job or cause a safety problem on the line. At home, my wife and kids get frustrated because I disrupt routines instead of helping them run smoothly.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 2 months\n  manifestation: It’s been this bad for about two years and two months, since my wife started working outside the home and I had to handle more of the schedule on my own.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 42 / production_worker / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S003\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is more with paying attention to details and following through on instructions, or with controlling your impulses and activities?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more the impulse side, if I’m being honest. I mean, I can have trouble with details here and there like anybody, but the thing that gets me in trouble the most is not stopping myself before I jump in.\n\nLike at work, if I see somebody doing a setup and I think, “That’s not right,” or “I can do that faster,” I don’t always wait for them to ask or finish what they’re doing. I’ll just reach in and start fixing something, or I’ll tell them how to do it while they’re still in the middle of doing their own job. And I know how that sounds — it sounds bossy, and it probably feels disrespectful to them — but in the moment it doesn’t feel like I’m trying to take over. It feels more like my body is already moving before I’ve had time to think, “Hold on, this isn’t your station, let them handle it.”\n\nThat’s where arguments start. They’ll say, “Back off, I’ve got it,” or get mad because I’m in their space, and then I get defensive because I feel like I was just trying to help or fix it quickly. But really, the issue is I didn’t wait my turn. I cut into what they were doing without being asked. So yeah, for me it’s more controlling that urge to jump in and act than it is following instructions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have more trouble sitting still and being patient, or do you have more trouble with forgetting things or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: Definitely more trouble with sitting still and being patient. I mean, I do make mistakes sometimes, sure, but the thing I really feel in my body is when I’m supposed to just sit there and wait.\n\nIt’s like this pressure starts building up inside me, especially in my chest and my legs. My legs feel like they need to move, and my chest gets this tight, restless feeling, like there’s energy trapped in there. And it’s not just “I’m a little bored.” It’s more like, if I don’t move or do something right now, I’m going to crawl out of my skin.\n\nSo if I’m sitting somewhere and I’m supposed to be calm, I’m usually fighting that feeling the whole time. I’m thinking about how much longer I have to stay there, or when I can get up, or what I can do with my hands or my feet. Being patient feels like holding something back physically, not just mentally.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself getting easily frustrated or angry in situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that one. I mean, I do get annoyed like anyone does, especially if something feels like it’s dragging on or not going the way I think it should, but I don’t know if I’d say I’m angry all the time or anything like that.\n\nWhen you say “easily frustrated,” I guess I’d need to think about what you mean exactly. Like, frustrated in the moment, yeah, that can happen. But I don’t know if I’ve really paid attention to it as its own thing, separate from just being impatient or wanting something to move along. Sometimes it’s hard for me to tell if I’m actually mad, or if I’m just wound up and reacting too fast.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say maybe, but I’m not sure. I haven’t really tracked it that way.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your difficulty with listening and following instructions is more because you're not paying attention or because you're too impatient to wait for the rest of the information?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s more like… I’m not waiting long enough to really take in the whole thing, if that makes sense. It’s not always that I’m staring off into space or don’t care what they’re saying. A lot of times they are talking right to me — my supervisor at work, or my wife at home — and I’m looking at them, I’m nodding, I think I’m listening.

But then a minute later I realize I only caught half of what they said. Like I’ll remember the first part, or the part that sounded most important right away, and then I’m already thinking about what I’m going to do or what I’m going to say back. Then they’ll say, “That’s not what I told you,” or “Did you even hear me?” And I’m standing there feeling stupid because I honestly thought I was listening.

That’s kind of what brought me in, really. People keep saying I’m not listening. My supervisor will give me instructions, or my wife will be explaining something, and then I have to ask them to repeat it because I missed pieces of it. I don’t mean to ignore them, but I don’t seem to stay with the information long enough to get the whole thing. So I guess it’s partly attention, but it feels tied to being too quick — like I jump ahead before they’re done.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, uh… I guess the main thing is people keep telling me I’m not listening. Like, not just once in a while—my supervisor at work will be standing right there talking straight to me, explaining something, and I’m looking at him like I’m hearing him, but then a minute later I realize I only caught pieces of it. Then I’m asking him to repeat himself, and it looks bad, like I don’t care or I’m ignoring him.\n\nAnd it’s the same at home with my wife. She’ll tell me something directly, and I honestly think I’m paying attention, but then afterward I realize I missed half of what she said. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be rude. It’s like the words are coming in, but they don’t all stick, if that makes sense. That’s really what pushed me to come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting, restless, or having trouble sitting still, especially in situations where you're supposed to be quiet or focused?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. That’s actually one of the things people notice about me all the time. If I’m sitting in one of those safety huddles at work, you know, where everyone’s supposed to just stand or sit there and listen, my leg is going the whole time. Like bouncing nonstop. And if I’m sitting down, I’ll be messing with a pen, clicking it, turning it around in my fingers, or I’ve got my keys in my hand and I don’t even realize I’m jingling them.\n\nSame thing at dinner, honestly. I’ll be at the table with my wife and my leg’s bouncing under the table, and she’ll be like, “Can you stop? You’re shaking everything.” Or if we’re sitting on a bench somewhere, people tell me I’m moving it because my foot keeps going. I don’t always notice I’m doing it until somebody points it out, but once they do, I realize I’ve been moving the whole time. It’s like sitting still takes effort, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble completing tasks or projects?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that one. I mean, everybody has stuff they don’t finish sometimes, right? If you mean like big projects where I just abandon them halfway through, I don’t know that I’ve really noticed a clear pattern like that.\n\nAt work, most things kind of have to get done because the line is moving or there’s a schedule, so it’s not like I can just walk away from it. And at home… I mean, I guess there are normal things that sit around sometimes, but I can’t say for sure it’s a major thing I’ve paid attention to. I’d have to think about what you mean exactly by “tasks or projects.”\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your time or activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Like, I don’t know if I’d call it “organizing my time” exactly. My days are pretty set because of work, so I kind of just go by the schedule that’s already there. If I’m supposed to be on the line or at a certain station, that sort of decides what I’m doing, so I’m not really sitting down and planning things out.\n\nAt home, I mean… I guess we have normal stuff, errands and chores and things like that, but I haven’t really thought of it as a big issue. My wife probably has a better idea of that than I do, because she pays more attention to the calendar and that kind of thing. I don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure. I guess I’d need you to explain what you mean—like being late, or forgetting plans, or not knowing what to do first? Because I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear pattern with that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they've finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I do that. Probably more than I’d like to admit. In meetings at work especially, like when the supervisor is asking something, I’ll jump in before he’s even finished the whole question. I’ll think I know where he’s going with it, and my mouth just kind of starts before I’ve really waited.\n\nAnd then sometimes I realize halfway through that he hadn’t even gotten to the important part yet. Like I’m already answering the first half, and then he adds something that changes what he was asking, and I feel kind of dumb because I basically cut him off for no reason. It’s not usually me trying to be rude or take over, it’s more like the answer is sitting right there and I feel this urge to get it out before I lose it or before somebody else says something. But yeah, I do interrupt that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting for your turn in conversations or activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not just in conversations, but at work especially, with activities or jobs on the line. I have a hard time waiting until it’s actually my turn to step in.\n\nLike if I see a coworker setting something up and I think they’re doing it too slow or not the way I would do it, I’ll just kind of reach in and start adjusting it, or I’ll tell them, “No, no, do it this way,” before they’ve asked me for help. And then it turns into a thing because, honestly, they’re right to get annoyed. It’s their station, their job at that moment, and I’m cutting into it like I’m in charge or like I can’t just wait and let them finish.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to start arguments. In my head it feels like, “I see the problem, I can fix it quick,” and then I’m already doing it before I’ve really thought about whether I should. But yeah, it causes problems. People snap at me, or I snap back, and then it becomes an argument because I didn’t wait my turn or stay out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is more with paying attention to details and following through on instructions, or with controlling your impulses and activities?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more the impulse side, if I’m being honest. I mean, I can have trouble with details here and there like anybody, but the thing that gets me in trouble the most is not stopping myself before I jump in.\n\nLike at work, if I see somebody doing a setup and I think, “That’s not right,” or “I can do that faster,” I don’t always wait for them to ask or finish what they’re doing. I’ll just reach in and start fixing something, or I’ll tell them how to do it while they’re still in the middle of doing their own job. And I know how that sounds — it sounds bossy, and it probably feels disrespectful to them — but in the moment it doesn’t feel like I’m trying to take over. It feels more like my body is already moving before I’ve had time to think, “Hold on, this isn’t your station, let them handle it.”\n\nThat’s where arguments start. They’ll say, “Back off, I’ve got it,” or get mad because I’m in their space, and then I get defensive because I feel like I was just trying to help or fix it quickly. But really, the issue is I didn’t wait my turn. I cut into what they were doing without being asked. So yeah, for me it’s more controlling that urge to jump in and act than it is following instructions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have more trouble sitting still and being patient, or do you have more trouble with forgetting things or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: Definitely more trouble with sitting still and being patient. I mean, I do make mistakes sometimes, sure, but the thing I really feel in my body is when I’m supposed to just sit there and wait.\n\nIt’s like this pressure starts building up inside me, especially in my chest and my legs. My legs feel like they need to move, and my chest gets this tight, restless feeling, like there’s energy trapped in there. And it’s not just “I’m a little bored.” It’s more like, if I don’t move or do something right now, I’m going to crawl out of my skin.\n\nSo if I’m sitting somewhere and I’m supposed to be calm, I’m usually fighting that feeling the whole time. I’m thinking about how much longer I have to stay there, or when I can get up, or what I can do with my hands or my feet. Being patient feels like holding something back physically, not just mentally.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself getting easily frustrated or angry in situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that one. I mean, I do get annoyed like anyone does, especially if something feels like it’s dragging on or not going the way I think it should, but I don’t know if I’d say I’m angry all the time or anything like that.\n\nWhen you say “easily frustrated,” I guess I’d need to think about what you mean exactly. Like, frustrated in the moment, yeah, that can happen. But I don’t know if I’ve really paid attention to it as its own thing, separate from just being impatient or wanting something to move along. Sometimes it’s hard for me to tell if I’m actually mad, or if I’m just wound up and reacting too fast.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say maybe, but I’m not sure. I haven’t really tracked it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with listening and following instructions is more because you're not paying attention or because you're too impatient to wait for the rest of the information?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more like… I’m not waiting long enough to really take in the whole thing, if that makes sense. It’s not always that I’m staring off into space or don’t care what they’re saying. A lot of times they are talking right to me — my supervisor at work, or my wife at home — and I’m looking at them, I’m nodding, I think I’m listening.\n\nBut then a minute later I realize I only caught half of what they said. Like I’ll remember the first part, or the part that sounded most important right away, and then I’m already thinking about what I’m going to do or what I’m going to say back. You’re shaking everything.” Or if we’re sitting on a bench somewhere, people tell me I’m moving it because my foot keeps going. I don’t always notice I’m doing it until somebody points it out, but once they do, I realize I’ve been moving the whole time. It’s like sitting still takes effort, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble completing tasks or projects?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that one. I mean, everybody has stuff they don’t finish sometimes, right? If you mean like big projects where I just abandon them halfway through, I don’t know that I’ve really noticed a clear pattern like that.\n\nAt work, most things kind of have to get done because the line is moving or there’s a schedule, so it’s not like I can just walk away from it. And at home… I mean, I guess there are normal things that sit around sometimes, but I can’t say for sure it’s a major thing I’ve paid attention to. I’d have to think about what you mean exactly by “tasks or projects.”\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your time or activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. Like, I don’t know if I’d call it “organizing my time” exactly. My days are pretty set because of work, so I kind of just go by the schedule that’s already there. If I’m supposed to be on the line or at a certain station, that sort of decides what I’m doing, so I’m not really sitting down and planning things out.\n\nAt home, I mean… I guess we have normal stuff, errands and chores and things like that, but I haven’t really thought of it as a big issue. My wife probably has a better idea of that than I do, because she pays more attention to the calendar and that kind of thing. I don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure. I guess I’d need you to explain what you mean—like being late, or forgetting plans, or not knowing what to do first? Because I’m not sure I’ve noticed a clear pattern with that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before they've finished speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I do that. Probably more than I’d like to admit. In meetings at work especially, like when the supervisor is asking something, I’ll jump in before he’s even finished the whole question. I’ll think I know where he’s going with it, and my mouth just kind of starts before I’ve really waited.\n\nAnd then sometimes I realize halfway through that he hadn’t even gotten to the important part yet. Like I’m already answering the first half, and then he adds something that changes what he was asking, and I feel kind of dumb because I basically cut him off for no reason. It’s not usually me trying to be rude or take over, it’s more like the answer is sitting right there and I feel this urge to get it out before I lose it or before somebody else says something. But yeah, I do interrupt that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble waiting for your turn in conversations or activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not just in conversations, but at work especially, with activities or jobs on the line. I have a hard time waiting until it’s actually my turn to step in.\n\nLike if I see a coworker setting something up and I think they’re doing it too slow or not the way I would do it, I’ll just kind of reach in and start adjusting it, or I’ll tell them, “No, no, do it this way,” before they’ve asked me for help. And then it turns into a thing because, honestly, they’re right to get annoyed. It’s their station, their job at that moment, and I’m cutting into it like I’m in charge or like I can’t just wait and let them finish.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying to start arguments. In my head it feels like, “I see the problem, I can fix it quick,” and then I’m already doing it before I’ve really thought about whether I should. But yeah, it causes problems. People snap at me, or I snap back, and then it becomes an argument because I didn’t wait my turn or stay out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is more with paying attention to details and following through on instructions, or with controlling your impulses and activities?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more the impulse side, if I’m being honest. I mean, I can have trouble with details here and there like anybody, but the thing that gets me in trouble the most is not stopping myself before I jump in.\n\nLike at work, if I see somebody doing a setup and I think, “That’s not right,” or “I can do that faster,” I don’t always wait for them to ask or finish what they’re doing. I’ll just reach in and start fixing something, or I’ll tell them how to do it while they’re still in the middle of doing their own job. And I know how that sounds — it sounds bossy, and it probably feels disrespectful to them — but in the moment it doesn’t feel like I’m trying to take over. It feels more like my body is already moving before I’ve had time to think, “Hold on, this isn’t your station, let them handle it.”\n\nThat’s where arguments start. They’ll say, “Back off, I’ve got it,” or get mad because I’m in their space, and then I get defensive because I feel like I was just trying to help or fix it quickly. But really, the issue is I didn’t wait my turn. I cut into what they were doing without being asked. So yeah, for me it’s more controlling that urge to jump in and act than it is following instructions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have more trouble sitting still and being patient, or do you have more trouble with forgetting things or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: Definitely more trouble with sitting still and being patient. I mean, I do make mistakes sometimes, sure, but the thing I really feel in my body is when I’m supposed to just sit there and wait.\n\nIt’s like this pressure starts building up inside me, especially in my chest and my legs. My legs feel like they need to move, and my chest gets this tight, restless feeling, like there’s energy trapped in there. And it’s not just “I’m a little bored.” It’s more like, if I don’t move or do something right now, I’m going to crawl out of my skin.\n\nSo if I’m sitting somewhere and I’m supposed to be calm, I’m usually fighting that feeling the whole time. I’m thinking about how much longer I have to stay there, or when I can get up, or what I can do with my hands or my feet. Being patient feels like holding something back physically, not just mentally.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself getting easily frustrated or angry in situations?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that one. I mean, I do get annoyed like anyone does, especially if something feels like it’s dragging on or not going the way I think it should, but I don’t know if I’d say I’m angry all the time or anything like that.\n\nWhen you say “easily frustrated,” I guess I’d need to think about what you mean exactly. Like, frustrated in the moment, yeah, that can happen. But I don’t know if I’ve really paid attention to it as its own thing, separate from just being impatient or wanting something to move along. Sometimes it’s hard for me to tell if I’m actually mad, or if I’m just wound up and reacting too fast.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say maybe, but I’m not sure. I haven’t really tracked it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulty with listening and following instructions is more because you're not paying attention or because you're too impatient to wait for the rest of the information?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more like… I’m not waiting long enough to really take in the whole thing, if that makes sense. It’s not always that I’m staring off into space or don’t care what they’re saying. A lot of times they are talking right to me — my supervisor at work, or my wife at home — and I’m looking at them, I’m nodding, I think I’m listening.\n\nBut then a minute later I realize I only caught half of what they said. Like I’ll remember the first part, or the part that sounded most important right away, and then I’m already thinking about what I’m going to do or what I’m going to say back. Then they’ll say, “That’s not what I told you,” or “Did you even hear me?” And I’m standing there feeling stupid because I honestly thought I was listening.\n\nThat’s kind of what brought me in, really. People keep saying I’m not listening. My supervisor will give me instructions, or my wife will be explaining something, and then I have to ask them to repeat it because I missed pieces of it. I don’t mean to ignore them, but I don’t seem to stay with the information long enough to get the whole thing. So I guess it’s partly attention, but it feels tied to being too quick — like I jump ahead before they’re done.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

{"intent": "Determine if the patient's symptoms are primarily related to attention deficit or impulse control", "question": "Do you think you would be able to focus and listen better if you were doing something that really interests you?"}

Yeah… I mean, maybe. If it’s something I’m really into, I probably do better, at least for a while. But the thing that made me come in is that even when I’m trying to listen, and even when the person is talking directly to me, I’m still missing pieces.
